**Lindsey Olin Graham**[^cite_note-2] (July 9, 1955 – July 11, 2026) was an American politician, attorney, and military officer who represented [South Carolina](27956.md) in the [United States Senate](24909346.md) from 2003 until his death in 2026. A member of the [Republican Party](32070.md), he represented [South Carolina's 3rd congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina's_3rd_congressional_district) in the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) from 1995 to 2003 and served in the [South Carolina House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_House_of_Representatives) from 1993 to 1995.

Born in [Central, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central,_South_Carolina), Graham graduated from the [University of South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_South_Carolina) and its law school. He served on active duty in the [United States Air Force](32090.md) from 1982 to 1989 as a member of the [Judge Advocate General's Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Air_Force_Judge_Advocate_General's_Corps), working as a defense attorney and as the Air Force's chief prosecutor in Europe. He later served in the [South Carolina Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Air_National_Guard) and the [United States Air Force Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Air_Force_Reserve), retiring as a colonel in 2015. He received the [Bronze Star Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Star_Medal) in 2014 for his legal work relating to military operations in Iraq and Afghanistan.

Graham was first elected to Congress in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_South_Carolina#3rd_congressional_district), drawing national attention as a member of the [House Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Judiciary_Committee) and as a House [impeachment manager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_manager) during the [impeachment trial of President Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_trial_of_Bill_Clinton). He was elected to the Senate in [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina) and reelected in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina), [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina), and [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina). He won the Republican nomination for a fifth term in [June 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina) but died before the general election. Governor [Henry McMaster](3482397.md) appointed Graham's sister, [Darline Graham](83683581.md), to serve out the remainder of Graham's fourth term.

A [war hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_hawk), Graham advocated an interventionist role for the United States and was a prominent supporter of [Israel](9282173.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), and the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization). He frequently worked with members of both parties, particularly on immigration and national-security legislation, and was closely associated with senators [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) and [Joe Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Lieberman), with whom he formed a group known as the "Three Amigos". Graham chaired the [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Judiciary_Committee) from 2019 to 2021 and the [Senate Budget Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Budget_Committee) from 2025 until his death. He also played a prominent role in the confirmation of President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s judicial nominees.

Graham [sought the Republican presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindsey_Graham_2016_presidential_campaign) in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries) but withdrew before the primaries began. Initially an outspoken critic of Trump during [Trump's 2016 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign), he later became one of Trump's closest congressional allies and supported him throughout both his presidencies.[^cite_note-Kim-2026-3][^cite_note-4]

## Early life

Lindsey Olin Graham was born on July 9, 1955, in [Central, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central,_South_Carolina), where his parents, Florence James "F.J." Graham and Millie Graham (née Walters), operated a restaurant, bar, liquor store, and pool hall. The family lived in rooms behind the business.[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7] His family was of [Scotch-Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans) descent.[^cite_note-Grove-1998-8][^cite_note-9]

After graduating from [D. W. Daniel High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._W._Daniel_High_School), Graham became the first member of his family to attend college and joined the [Reserve Officers' Training Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Officers'_Training_Corps). When he was 20, his mother died of [Hodgkin's lymphoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hodgkin's_lymphoma) at age 50, and his father died 15 months later of a [heart attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_attack) at age 68.[^cite_note-Grove-1998-8] Because his sister [Darline](83683581.md), then 13 years old, was left orphaned, the service allowed Graham to attend the [University of South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_South_Carolina) in [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia,_South_Carolina) so he could remain near home as his sister's [legal guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_guardian).[^cite_note-Ball-2014-10] During his studies, he became a member of the [Pi Kappa Phi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pi_Kappa_Phi) social [fraternity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraternity).[^cite_note-11]

Graham said his parents' early deaths made him mature more quickly and Darline, who introduced her brother at his 2016 announcement of his candidacy for president, said she hoped to be with him on the campaign trail frequently to show voters his softer side. "He's kind of like a brother, a father and a mother rolled into one", she said. "I've always looked up to Lindsey."[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13]

Graham graduated from the University of South Carolina with a [Bachelor of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts) in [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology) in 1977. He obtained his [Juris Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juris_Doctor) from the [University of South Carolina School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_South_Carolina_School_of_Law) in 1981.[^cite_note-14]

## U.S. Air Force officer (1982–2015)



Lt. Gen. [Jack L. Rives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_L._Rives) pins the [Meritorious Service Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meritorious_Service_Medal_\(United_States\)) on [Colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonel_\(United_States\)) Lindsey Graham, April 2009

Upon graduating from the University of South Carolina School of Law, Graham was commissioned as an officer in the [Judge Advocate General's Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Air_Force_Judge_Advocate_General's_Corps) (JAG Corps) in the [United States Air Force](32090.md) in 1982 and began active duty that year. His military service began as an Air Force defense attorney. He was stationed at [Shaw Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaw_Air_Force_Base) from 1982 to 1984. He was later transferred to [Rhein-Main Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhein-Main_Air_Base) in [Frankfurt](10992.md), [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany), where he served as the Air Force's chief prosecutor in Europe from 1984 to 1988. In 1984, as he was defending an Air Force pilot accused of using [marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marijuana), he was featured in an episode of *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)* that exposed the Air Force's defective drug-testing procedures.[^cite_note-Grove-1998-8][^cite_note-BaltimoreSun-2004-15][^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16] After his service in Europe, he returned to South Carolina, leaving active duty in 1989[^cite_note-bio-17] and entering a private law practice in [Walhalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walhalla,_South_Carolina) with Larry Brandt.[^cite_note-Grove-1998-8][^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16] He served as assistant county attorney for [Oconee County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oconee_County,_South_Carolina) from 1988 to 1992. In 1992, he won a \$5 million medical malpractice case that awarded him US\$200,000 in split-earnings. He was a city attorney for [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central,_South_Carolina) from 1990 to 1994.[^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16][^cite_note-18]

After leaving the Air Force, Graham joined the [South Carolina Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Air_National_Guard) in 1989, where he served until 1995, then joining the [U.S. Air Force Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Air_Force_Reserve).[^cite_note-bio-17]

In 1998, the [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill) daily newspaper *[The Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_\(newspaper\))* contended that Graham was describing himself on his website as an [Operation Desert Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Desert_Shield) and [Desert Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert_Storm) veteran. Graham responded: "I have not told anybody I'm a combatant. I'm not a war hero, and never said I was. ... If I have lied about my military record, I'm not fit to serve in Congress", further noting that he "never deployed".[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

In 1998, Graham was promoted to [lieutenant colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Colonel_\(United_States\)). In 2004, he received his promotion to [colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonel_\(United_States\)) in the U.S. Air Force Reserve at a [White House](33057.md) ceremony officiated by President [George W. Bush](3414021.md).[^cite_note-Whitlock-2015-21] That year, a lower court determined that Graham's service as a military judge while a sitting member of the Senate was acceptable. In 2006, the [Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Armed_Forces) set aside the lower court's ruling after concluding it was improper for Graham to serve as a military judge.[^cite_note-22]

In 2007, Graham served in [Iraq](7515928.md) as a reservist on active duty for a short period in April and for two weeks in August, where he worked on detainee and [rule of law](25166191.md) issues.[^cite_note-23] He also served in [Afghanistan](737.md) during the August 2009 Senate recess.[^cite_note-24] He was then assigned as a senior instructor at the [Judge Advocate General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge_Advocate_General)'s School, though he never went.[^cite_note-Whitlock-2015-21]

In 2014, Graham received a [Bronze Star Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Star_Medal) for meritorious service as a senior legal adviser to the Air Force in Iraq and Afghanistan from August 2009 to July 2014, overseeing the detention of military prisoners.[^cite_note-Collins-2026-25][^cite_note-26][^cite_note-Whitlock-2015-21] In 2015, he retired at his last rank of [colonel](199023.md) from the Air Force with over 33 total years of service, after reaching the statutory retirement age of 60 for his rank.[^cite_note-27] Graham earned points toward a military pension but was unpaid as an Air Force officer while a member of Congress as he was ineligible for a military paycheck during his time in federal government service.[^cite_note-Whitlock-2015-21]

## South Carolina House of Representatives (1992–1994)

In 1992, Graham was elected to the [South Carolina House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_House_of_Representatives) from the 2nd district, in Oconee County. He defeated Democratic incumbent, [Lowell W. Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowell_W._Ross), 60% to 40%,[^cite_note-\:0-28] and served one term, from 1993 to 1995.[^cite_note-29] His first bill in the House sought to ban [gays and lesbians](78174325.md) from service in the [South Carolina Army National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Army_National_Guard). It did not pass.[^cite_note-30] He proposed a bill that would impose a limit of 12 consecutive years for a state legislator. He served on the Judiciary Committee.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

## U.S. House of Representatives (1995–2003)

### Elections

In 1994, 20-year incumbent Democratic U.S. congressman [Butler Derrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butler_Derrick) of South Carolina's northwestern-based [3rd congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina's_3rd_congressional_district) decided to retire.[^cite_note-\:0-28] Graham ran to succeed him and, with Republican U.S. senator [Strom Thurmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strom_Thurmond) campaigning on his behalf, won the Republican primary with 52% of the vote, defeating Bob Cantrell (33%) and Ed Allgood (15%).[^cite_note-SCEC-1995-33]<sup>: 18 </sup> In the general election, Graham defeated Democratic State Senator James Bryan Jr., 60% to 40%.[^cite_note-SCEC-1995-33]<sup>: 29 </sup> As a part of that year's [Republican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Revolution), Graham campaigned in support of [Newt Gingrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newt_Gingrich)'s [Contract with America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contract_with_America) legislative agenda and became the first Republican to represent the district since 1877.[^cite_note-Draper-2010-34][^cite_note-Welch-2002-35]

In 1996, he was challenged by Debbie Dorn, the niece of Butler Derrick and daughter of Derrick's predecessor, 13-term Democratic congressman [William Jennings Bryan Dorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan_Dorn). Graham was reelected, defeating Dorn 60% to 40%.[^cite_note-36] In 1998, he was reelected to a third term unopposed.[^cite_note-37] In 2000, he was reelected to a fourth term, defeating Democratic nominee George Brightharp, 68% to 30%.[^cite_note-38]

### Tenure

Graham speaking during Clinton's impeachment on December 18, 1998, and also discussing the grounds for impeachment of [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) and other presidents

In 1994, Graham, along with other Republicans elected that year, promised to serve no more than 12 years in office.[^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16] In 1995, he was one of five Republicans to vote against settling a budget standoff that caused a [government shutdown](2150997.md).[^cite_note-Welch-2002-35] In 1997, he took part in [a leadership challenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newt_Gingrich#Leadership_challenge) against [House Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) Newt Gingrich.[^cite_note-Grove-1998-8][^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16]

In November 1997, Graham was one of 18 House Republicans to co-sponsor [a resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_impeachment_resolution_by_Bob_Barr) by [Bob Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Barr) that sought to launch an [impeachment inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_in_the_United_States) into President [Bill Clinton](3356.md).[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-Hutcheson-1997-40] The resolution did not specify any charges or allegations.[^cite_note-Hutcheson-1997-40] This was an early effort to [impeach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_impeachment_in_the_United_States) Clinton, predating the eruption of the [Clinton–Lewinsky scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton%E2%80%93Lewinsky_scandal). The eruption of that scandal ultimately led to a more serious effort to impeach Clinton in 1998.[^cite_note-41] On October 8, 1998, Graham voted in favor of legislation to open [an impeachment inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_against_Bill_Clinton).[^cite_note-Ball-2014-10][^cite_note-42] He was a member of the [Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_the_Judiciary), which conducted the inquiry.[^cite_note-43]

In both the Judiciary Committee vote on forwarding proposed [articles of impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_impeachment), and the full House vote on the proposed articles of impeachment, Graham voted for three of the four proposed articles of impeachment. He voted against the second count of perjury in the [Paula Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paula_Jones) case.[^cite_note-RC546-44][^cite_note-RC545-45][^cite_note-RC544-46][^cite_note-RC543-47] This made him the only Republican on the Judiciary Committee to vote against any of the proposed articles of impeachment.[^cite_note-Ball-2014-10] During the inquiry, Graham asked, "Is this [Watergate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate) or [Peyton Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peyton_Place_\(TV_series\))?"[^cite_note-Ball-2014-10][^cite_note-Grove-1998-8] The House [passed two of the impeachment articles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Bill_Clinton).[^cite_note-RC546-44][^cite_note-RC545-45][^cite_note-RC544-46][^cite_note-RC543-47] Graham served as a [House impeachment manager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_impeachment_manager) in [the impeachment trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_trial_of_Bill_Clinton).[^cite_note-48]

In 1999 and 2001, Graham sponsored the Unborn Victims of Violence Act in the House. It passed by margins of 254–172 and 252–172 in 1999 and 2001, respectively. The bill sought to make it a federal crime to harm a fetus during an assault of its mother, except by medical professionals with the mother's consent.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] In both cases, the Senate did not take up the bill for a vote. In 2004, the similar [Unborn Victims of Violence Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unborn_Victims_of_Violence_Act) was signed into law.[^cite_note-51]

After the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), Graham voted for the [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act) and the [Authorization for Use of Military Force of 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_of_2001).[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] In 2002, Graham voted for the [Iraq resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_resolution).[^cite_note-54]

### Committee assignments

During his service in the House, Graham had served on the following committees:

- [Committee on International Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_International_Relations) (1995–1998)

  [^cite_note-HouseCommittees-55]

- [Committee on Education and the Workforce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_Education_and_the_Workforce) (1995–2002)

  [^cite_note-HouseCommittees-55]

- [Committee on the Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) (1997–2002)

  [^cite_note-56]

- [Committee on National Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_Armed_Services) (1999–2002)

  [^cite_note-57]

## U.S. Senate (2003–2026)



Official portrait, 2006

### Elections

#### 2002

Main article: [2002 United States Senate election in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina)

In 2001, [Bob Dole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dole) signed on as the chairman of the national steering committee for Graham's campaign for the U.S. Senate.[^cite_note-58] In 2002, longtime U.S. senator [Strom Thurmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strom_Thurmond) decided to retire.[^cite_note-\:0-28] Graham ran to succeed him and won the Republican primary unopposed. In the general election, he defeated Democratic nominee [Alex Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Sanders_\(politician\)), former president of the [College of Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Charleston) and former chief judge of the [South Carolina Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Court_of_Appeals), 54.40% to 44.19%.[^cite_note-59] Before the election, Graham promised to serve no more than two six-year terms in office.[^cite_note-Markoe-2002-16] He became South Carolina's first new U.S. senator since [Fritz Hollings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Hollings) in 1966.[^cite_note-60]

#### 2008

Main article: [2008 United States Senate election in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina)

Graham ran for a second term in 2008, and was challenged in the Republican primary by National Executive Committeeman of the [South Carolina Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Republican_Party), Buddy Witherspoon. Graham won renomination, 66.82% to 33.18%, winning all but one of South Carolina's 46 counties.[^cite_note-61]

In the general election, Graham defeated Democratic nominee Bob Conley, a pilot and engineer, 57.53% to 42.25%. He outspent Conley, \$6.6 million to \$15,000.[^cite_note-62]

#### 2014

Main article: [2014 United States Senate election in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina)

Graham ran for reelection in 2014. Of the Republican senators up for reelection that year, he was considered one of the most vulnerable to a primary challenge, largely due to his low approval ratings and reputation for working with and compromising with Democrats.[^cite_note-Kludt-2014-63][^cite_note-64] He expected a primary challenge from conservative activists, including the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement), and [Chris Chocola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Chocola), President of the [Club for Growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Club_for_Growth), said his organization would support a primary challenge if an acceptable standard-bearer emerged.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66]

But no serious challenger to Graham emerged, and he was widely viewed as likely to win,[^cite_note-Killian-2014-67][^cite_note-Murphy-2014-68][^cite_note-Kludt-2014-63] which has been ascribed to his "deft maneuvering" and "aggressive" response to the challenge. He befriended potential opponents from the state's congressional delegation and helped them with fundraising and securing their preferred committee assignments. He assembled a "daunting multi-million-dollar political operation" dubbed the "Graham machine" that built six regional offices across the state and enlisted the support of thousands of paid staffers and volunteers, including over 5,000 [precinct captains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precinct_captain); built a "staggering" campaign war chest and "blanketed" the state with ads; focused on constituent services and local issues; and refused to "pander" to Tea Party supporters, instead confronting them head-on, arguing that the Republican Party needed to be more inclusive.[^cite_note-Raju-2014-69][^cite_note-Draper-2010-34][^cite_note-Ball-2014-10][^cite_note-Murphy-2014-68][^cite_note-70]

In the run-up to the Republican primary, Graham's approval rating improved. A February 2013 Winthrop poll found that he had a 59% positive rating among likely Republican voters.[^cite_note-71]

In June 2014, Graham won the Republican primary with 56.42% of the vote in a crowded field. His closest challenger, State Senator [Lee Bright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Bright), received 15.4%. His other primary opponents included [Richard Cash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Cash_\(politician\)) and [Nancy Mace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Mace).[^cite_note-72]

In the general election, Graham defeated the Democratic nominee, State Senator [Brad Hutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Hutto), 54% to 39%. Independent [Thomas Ravenel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Ravenel) (a former Republican State Treasurer), and Libertarian Victor Kocher received 3.8% and 2.7% of the vote, respectively.[^cite_note-73]

#### 2020

Main article: [2020 United States Senate election in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina)

Graham ran for reelection in 2020, winning renomination in the Republican primary with 67.69% of the vote against three other candidates.[^cite_note-74]

Democrat [Jaime Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Harrison) challenged Graham in the general election.[^cite_note-75] The race was considered potentially competitive, with several polls in its final months showing a close race and Harrison posting record fundraising numbers, but Graham won by more than 10 points, 54.4% to 44.2%.[^cite_note-76]

#### 2026

Main article: [2026 United States Senate election in South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina)

Graham ran for reelection in 2026. On January 14, 2025, he announced lead officials for his campaign, including the former chairman of the South Carolina Republican Party and Governor [Henry McMaster](3482397.md)'s campaign manager.[^cite_note-77] He reported \$15.6 million in campaign fundraising. Trump endorsed Graham on March 26, 2025.[^cite_note-78]

On June 9, 2026, Graham won the Republican primary with 56.8% of the vote against five other candidates.[^cite_note-NYT-2026-79] Graham died on July 11, and was replaced by [Darline Graham](83683581.md), who was replaced as the Republican nominee in a special primary.[^cite_note-\:1-80]

At the time of his death, Graham was [running for reelection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_Senate_election_in_South_Carolina) to a fifth Senate term as the Republican nominee. He had won the Republican primary with 56.8% of the vote and the [2026 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_midterm_elections) were 114 days away.[^cite_note-NYT-2026-79] On July 13, South Carolina Governor [Henry McMaster](3482397.md) appointed Graham's sister, [Darline Graham](83683581.md), to serve the remainder of his fourth term.[^cite_note-81] [Darline Graham](83683581.md) then went on to win the special primary runoff, and was nominated as the Republican nominee.[^cite_note-\:1-80]

At the time of his death, Graham served on these committees:

- [Committee on Appropriations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Appropriations)

- [Committee on Environment and Public Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_Environment_and_Public_Works)

  [^cite_note-82]

- [Budget Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Budget_Committee) ([chair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman)).

Graham was a member of the board of directors of the [International Republican Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Republican_Institute).[^cite_note-85]

### Relationship with Donald Trump



Graham and [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in January 2019

In July 2015, when Graham was a presidential candidate, he called [Donald Trump](4848272.md), then another presidential candidate, a "jackass" for saying that Graham's close friend, Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain), was "not a war hero". Trump reacted by calling Graham an "idiot" and revealing Graham's personal cellphone number at a campaign rally, asking people to call Graham.[^cite_note-86]

In December 2015, Trump, the front-runner for the Republican presidential nomination, called for a ban on [Muslims](19541.md) entering the United States. Graham, who had very little support as a presidential candidate, responded: "He's a race-baiting, xenophobic, religious bigot ... He doesn't represent my \[Republican] party ... I don't think he has a clue about anything ... He is empowering radical Islam ... You know how you make America great again? Tell Donald Trump to go to hell." He added, "I'd rather lose without Donald Trump than try to win with him."[^cite_note-87]

In May 2016 Graham tweeted, "If we nominate Trump, we will get destroyed...and we will deserve it."[^cite_note-89]

In June 2016, after Trump criticized a judge of Mexican heritage, implying he could be biased, Graham said to [CNN](62028.md): "I don't think \[Trump is] racist but he's playing the race card ... I think it's very un-American ... If he continues this line of attack then I think people really need to reconsider the future of the \[Republican] party." Graham told *The New York Times* that that incident "is probably it" for anyone looking to withdraw their support of Trump: "There'll come a time when the love of country will trump hatred of Hillary Clinton", the Democratic nominee for president.[^cite_note-90]

In the November presidential election, Graham did not vote for Trump, saying, "I couldn't go where Donald Trump wanted to take the USA & GOP." He voted for independent candidate [Evan McMullin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_McMullin).[^cite_note-Wang-2019-91]

After a March 2017 meeting with Trump, Graham became a vocal ally of his, often issuing public statements in his defense and catching members of both parties by surprise. Graham said that the meeting went so well that he passed his new phone number to Trump, in reference to their 2015 conflict.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-Feit-2017-93] Commentators have noted that Graham's public reconciliation with Trump coincided with his expanded influence on foreign policy, supporting military interventions, increased [defense spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_defense_spending), and a more aggressive U.S. role abroad.[^cite_note-94]

In October 2017, Graham and Trump played golf together on multiple occasions, with Graham praising the first outing.[^cite_note-Feit-2017-93] In November 2017, Graham criticized the media's reporting on Trump: "What concerns me about the American press is this endless, endless attempt to label the guy some kind of kook not fit to be president." (In February 2016, Graham said of Trump: "I think he's a kook. I think he's crazy. I think he's unfit for office.")[^cite_note-Kwong-2019-95][^cite_note-96]

In April 2018, Graham said that he would support Trump's reelection in 2020.[^cite_note-97] In January 2019, Graham said that Republicans must support Trump's policies: "If we undercut the president, that's the end of his presidency and the end of our party."[^cite_note-98]



Graham and Donald Trump in February 2026

In February 2019 [Mark Leibovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Leibovich) interviewed Graham for *[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)*. He asked Graham how he became a prominent Trump supporter. Graham responded that he was attempting "to be relevant": "I've got an opportunity up here working with the president to get some really good outcomes for the country ... I have never been called this much by a president in my life ... He's asked me to do some things, and I've asked him to do some things in return." Graham said he had been gaining influence with Trump and was attempting to enter Trump's inner circle, where he would reach a level of influence on par with [Melania Trump](1209075.md), [Ivanka Trump](1861441.md), and [Jared Kushner](6191053.md). He said that he had had a "political marriage" with John McCain, but as for his relationship with Trump: "I personally like him. We play golf. He's very nice to me." Graham also said that a good relationship with Trump would help his prospects of reelection to the Senate in 2020.[^cite_note-99]

Seven months after the death of McCain, one of Graham's "dearest friends",[^cite_note-100] Trump repeatedly criticized McCain. Graham was then criticized for not standing up for McCain. Graham responded, "To all those people who bring up this narrative, you just hate Trump ... You're not offended about me and McCain; you're trying to use me to get to Trump ... I'm not into this idea that the only way to honor John McCain is to trash out Trump." He also said, "The bottom line here is I'm going to help President Trump."[^cite_note-101] McCain had banned Trump from his funeral. Trump's daughter Ivanka attended his funeral, reportedly at the invitation of Graham, who had reportedly gotten McCain's wife's permission.[^cite_note-102] According to Graham, Trump called him after he delivered an emotional farewell to McCain on the Senate floor, telling him he "did right by his friend."[^cite_note-103]

On May 14, 2019, Graham came under scrutiny, including from Senator [Joe Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin), after encouraging [Donald Trump Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_Jr.) to ignore a subpoena delivered by the [Senate Intelligence Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Intelligence_Committee).[^cite_note-104]

In July 2019, Graham said he did not think Trump was racist and that he did not think that Trump's statements that certain Democratic congresswomen should "go back and help fix the totally broken and crime infested places from which they came" were racist. Graham said, "I don't think a Somali refugee embracing Trump would be asked to go back. If you're racist, you want everybody to go back because they are black or Muslim."[^cite_note-Kwong-2019-95] Earlier in August 2018, *The Washington Post* reported that Graham had said, "I have never heard him make a single racist statement. Not even close."[^cite_note-105]

On October 8, 2019, during an interview with [Jonathan Swan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Swan) of [Axios](52436164.md), Graham condemned Trump's announcement of an intention to withdraw United States troops from northern Syria, saying that Trump was putting the nation and his presidency at risk, and that it was without the support of key national security advisers.[^cite_note-106] Media noted Graham's reversals and Trump's apparent lack of appreciation for his advice.[^cite_note-107]

In December 2019, as two articles of impeachment against Trump moved to a vote before the full House and referral to the Senate for trial, Graham said, "I am trying to give a pretty clear signal I have made up my mind. I'm not trying to pretend to be a fair juror here", adding, "this thing will come to the Senate, and it will die quickly, and I will do everything I can to make it die quickly."[^cite_note-108] He also announced that he held "disdain for the accusations and the process. So I don't need any witnesses" for the Senate trial.[^cite_note-109] In response, Democrats referenced statements Graham made during the 1998 impeachment of Bill Clinton, including his citation of [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) as proof that a president who ignored a subpoena should be impeached for taking "the power from Congress over the impeachment process away from Congress" and becoming "judge and jury" himself.[^cite_note-110]

In April 2024, Graham criticized Trump for not supporting a federal abortion ban. In reply, Trump expressed regret for endorsing Graham in his 2020 Senate campaign.[^cite_note-111]

In January 2025, Graham took issue with a series of Trump's first actions as president. Graham said it was "a mistake" for Trump to pardon criminal defendants who pleaded guilty to or were convicted of violent crimes during the [January 6 attack on the Capitol](66307490.md), citing concerns about condoning violence and undermining support of law enforcement officers.[^cite_note-NBC-2025-112] Graham said Congress should "revisit" the scope of the [presidential pardon power](54636577.md) in light of the [January 6 pardons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_pardons) and President [Joe Biden](145422.md)'s pardons for his family members.[^cite_note-NBC-2025-112] Next, Graham disagreed with Trump's decision to remove the security details provided to former National Security Advisor [John Bolton](997107.md) and former Secretary of State [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo).[^cite_note-113] Lastly, Graham defended Trump's decision to fire 17 [Inspectors General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspectors_General), but criticized Trump for "technically" violating the law by not giving Congress 30 days' notice of the firings.[^cite_note-114]

#### Reaction to 2020 presidential election results

See also: [Disputes surrounding the 2020 United States presidential election results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disputes_surrounding_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election_results)

After all major news networks projected that [Joe Biden](145422.md) had won the [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md), Graham said that Trump "should not concede" because "if Republicans don't challenge and change the U.S. election system, there will never be another Republican president elected again".[^cite_note-115] Graham said he donated \$500,000 to Trump's election lawsuits in various states,[^cite_note-116] and that the option should be "on the table" for Republican state legislators to invalidate election results due to alleged "corruption" by appointing presidential electors who would vote for Trump.[^cite_note-117]

After receiving an affidavit by [Pennsylvania](23332.md) postal worker Richard Hopkins alleging that his postmaster discussed backdating mail ballots, Graham issued a statement that "all credible allegations of voting irregularities and misconduct be investigated to ensure the integrity of the 2020 elections", including Hopkins's.[^cite_note-118] Hopkins's affidavit was released by [Project Veritas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Veritas), a controversial conservative organization known for using deceptive tactics; Project Veritas later released a recording in which Hopkins says that he did not hear his postmaster explicitly discuss backdating ballots, and that Project Veritas wrote his affidavit for him.[^cite_note-119]

The [2020 United States presidential election in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia) produced an initial count where Biden defeated Trump by around 14,000 votes, triggering a recount due to the small margin.[^cite_note-Garder-2020-120] During the recount, Graham privately called the [Georgia Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Secretary_of_State), [Brad Raffensperger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Raffensperger), to discuss Georgia's vote counting.[^cite_note-Garder-2020-120] Raffensperger, a Republican, told *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* that Graham had asked Raffensperger whether Raffensperger could disqualify all mail-in ballots in counties with more signature errors.[^cite_note-Garder-2020-120] [Gabriel Sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Sterling), a Republican election official and staffer to Raffensperger, was present on the call; Sterling confirmed that Graham had asked that question.[^cite_note-121] Raffensperger viewed Graham's question as a suggestion to throw out legally cast ballots. Graham denied suggesting this.[^cite_note-Garder-2020-120] Graham acknowledged calling Raffensperger to find out how to "protect the integrity of mail-in voting" and "how does signature verification work", but said that if Raffensperger "feels threatened by that conversation, he's got a problem".[^cite_note-Garder-2020-120] Graham said that he was investigating in his capacity as a senator, although he was the chair of the Senate Judiciary Committee.[^cite_note-Kane-2020-122] He went on to claim that he had also spoken to Arizona's and Nevada's secretaries of state.[^cite_note-Kane-2020-122] Those secretaries denied this, and Graham reversed himself, saying that he had spoken to the governor of Arizona and no official in Nevada.[^cite_note-Kane-2020-122] *The Washington Post* reported in February 2021 that [Fani Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fani_Willis), the [Fulton County, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulton_County,_Georgia) district attorney, was examining Graham's phone call to Raffensperger as part of a criminal investigation into possible efforts to illegally overturn Georgia's election results.[^cite_note-123]

On January 6, 2021, Graham, [Vice President Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_Mike_Pence), and members of the Senate and House were evacuated from the Capitol building after Trump supporters [attacked the United States Capitol](66307490.md). The [joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_Congress) reconvened late into the night and the early morning in the Senate chamber to count and confirm the Electoral College votes. Graham spoke, disagreeing with many of his Republican colleagues, [who mostly supported Trump's denials of the election's results](65776878.md), saying, "it's a uniquely bad idea to delay this election", and though "I hate it", they could "count me out, enough is enough". He finished by saying, "Joe Biden and [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) are lawfully elected and will become the President and the Vice President of the United States on January the 20th."[^cite_note-124]

In the resulting [second impeachment trial of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_trial_of_Donald_Trump), Graham voted "not guilty".[^cite_note-125]

On May 28, 2021, Graham voted against creating the [January 6 commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_commission).[^cite_note-126]

In August 2021, *The New York Times* reported that Graham called Biden days after the election in an effort to revive their friendship and told Biden he had called for a [special counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_counsel) investigation of Biden's son [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden) during the campaign only to appease Trump supporters among his constituents. A Graham spokesman disputed the *Times*' account.[^cite_note-127]

#### 2024 election

Graham appeared at Trump's first prime-time [2024 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign) rally on January 28, 2023, and told [Fox News](11121.md) host [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity) that he would support Trump "because I know what I'm going to get", mostly regarding perceived international threats.[^cite_note-128] Graham campaigned and publicly advocated for Trump on news programs like *[Meet the Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meet_the_Press)* throughout the 2024 election.[^cite_note-NBC-2024-129] He also called on other prominent Republican politicians to campaign for Trump.[^cite_note-NBC-2024-129]

In May 2024, when asked whether he would accept the results of the presidential election, Graham said he would "if there's no massive cheating".[^cite_note-130]

In July 2024, after President Biden announced he would not seek reelection, Graham said he wished Biden well and that he "appreciate\[d] his lifelong service to our nation".[^cite_note-131]

In September 2024, with growing concern about a tie in the [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College), Graham visited Nebraska—one of two states without a [winner-take-all vote allocation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winner-take-all_system). He lobbied Governor [Jim Pillen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Pillen) to call a special session of the legislature to adopt the winner-take-all system, preventing Kamala Harris from receiving the state's one Electoral College vote typically won by Democrats.[^cite_note-Lotz-2024-132] Trump endorsed the change. The move failed when too few state senators agreed to call a special session.[^cite_note-Lotz-2024-132][^cite_note-133]

In October 2024, Graham said Republican voters who supported Harris were supporting "the most radical nominee in history of American politics".[^cite_note-134] He argued that the Biden-Harris immigration policy was a larger "danger to this country" than Trump's rhetoric.[^cite_note-135]

Graham called Trump's 2024 election victory "the biggest comeback in American history".[^cite_note-136]
